It’s like I said about missing my doctor visits, I just can't keep it straight. \n\nSpeaking of the plumber, we had the most awful leak under the kitchen sink last Tuesday. The water just went everywhere, completely soaked the linoleum. We've had that same linoleum since 1985. It’s this lovely pale yellow color with little blue flowers on it. My mother hated it, she always said yellow belongs in a nursery, not a kitchen. But I always thought it looked so cheerful when the morning sun came through the window over the sink. I used to stand there washing dishes and watching the robins pull worms out of the front lawn. They're such clever little birds, robins. Did you know they can actually hear the worms moving underground? It’s true, I read it in a magazine once... a National Geographic, I think. Or maybe it was Reader's Digest. I used to have a subscription to Reader's Digest, they always had the best little jokes in the back...\n\nI'm sorry, dear, what did you ask me about? The checkbook? Yes, my husband does all of that now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or worried?\n\nPatient: Nervous? No, I wouldn't say nervous. I mean, everyone worries a little bit, don't they? Like when my grandson started driving. Oh, I was a wreck then! He bought this old beat-up Honda Civic, it was bright red. Well, it used to be bright red, the paint on the hood was peeling off in these big ugly flakes. He loved that car, though. He spent every weekend under the hood, getting grease all over his hands. His mother was always yelling at him about tracking the grease into the house. She had just put down this beautiful cream-colored carpet in the hallway. Cream! Can you imagine? With a teenager and two golden retrievers in the house? I told her it was a mistake, but she never listens to me. She’s stubborn, just like her father. My husband was the exact same way, once he made up his mind about something, that was it. Like the time he decided we were going to drive all the way to Mount Rushmore... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? You were asking if I feel nervous. No, dear, not really nervous. Just... foggy, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Has this memory problem been getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Well, getting worse... I suppose it's been about four months now. It all started right around the time we had that terrible water damage. You know, from the leak under the sink I was telling you about? It ruined the cabinets, too, so we were pushed right into a remodel. \n\nAnd let me tell you, remodeling a kitchen is no joke. The dust! It gets everywhere, no matter how many plastic sheets they put up. We had these contractors in and out for weeks. The main fellow, Frank, he was a nice enough man, but he always wore these heavy work boots that left scuff marks all over the hardwood in the dining room. I tried to ask him to take them off, but my husband said it was a safety hazard for them to work in their socks. I suppose he was right, but it took me ages to get those scuffs out. I used to use this wonderful polish, Pledge, I think it was. The one in the yellow can that smells like lemons? They don't make it the same way anymore, though. Nothing smells quite like it used to. Even the lemons at the grocery store seem a bit... dull, don't you think? I remember when a lemon would just fill the whole kitchen with its scent...\n\nOh, dear, I've lost the thread again. What was it? Yes, four months. That's how long it's been like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with dressing or bathing?\n\nPatient: No, no, I can dress myself just fine. And bathe, yes. I still take my baths every evening. I've always preferred a bath to a shower, you know. Showers just feel so rushed. I like to fill the tub up nice and warm and use those little bath oil beads. Do you remember those? They used to come in all sorts of shapes, like little pearls or dolphins, and they'd melt in the hot water. My daughter bought me this big jar of them for Mother's Day back in... oh, it must have been 1998. It was a beautiful glass jar with a cork stopper. I kept it on the edge of the tub for years, even after the beads were all gone. I ended up putting cotton balls in it eventually. Cotton balls are so useful, aren't they? I use them for everything. Taking off makeup, cleaning the silver... my grandmother taught me that trick with the silver. She had this massive collection of silver spoons from all over the world. Every time someone went on a trip, they had to bring her back a spoon. She had one from Paris with a tiny little Eiffel Tower on the handle, and one from London with a guard in a red coat...\n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again, aren't I? You were asking about getting dressed. Yes, I manage all of that on my own.\n\nDoctor: Did this confusion start suddenly after the water leak and kitchen remodel?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Well, I don't know if I'd say suddenly. It's hard to put a finger on the exact day, you know? It just sort of... crept up on me while all that mess was happening. I remember standing in the kitchen, right in the middle of all that dust I told you about, trying to figure out where the contractor had moved my good mixing bowls. They were these beautiful Pyrex bowls, the vintage ones? Primary colors. The large one was yellow, then green, red, and the littlest one was blue. I got them as a wedding present from my Aunt Clara. She was such a practical woman. Always gave gifts you could actually use, not like those decorative porcelain figurines my mother-in-law used to collect. She had dozens of those little shepherdesses taking up every inch of the mantelpiece. Dust collectors, I called them. And they were a nightmare to clean. You had to take a little soft-bristled toothbrush to get into all the tiny folds of their porcelain dresses... \n\nOh, what was I saying? The confusion. Yes, I'm just not sure if it was sudden. It just seemed to happen around that whole kitchen ordeal.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop driving because of these memory problems?\n\nPatient: Driving? Oh, well, I still drive a little bit, mostly just to the grocery store and back. But it's all the other things that have gotten so tangled up. I missed two doctor's appointments last month, just completely forgot them. And the schedule at the senior center... I used to organize the Tuesday bridge club, you know. We’ve been playing for fifteen years. We play Chicago style, which I always say is much more forgiving if you draw a bad partner. My friend Helen, bless her heart, she bids like she’s got eyes in the back of her head, but she can’t remember what trump is half the time. She always brings these little lemon bars to the games, though. The ones with the powdered sugar on top? They are just divine. I asked her for the recipe once, and she wrote it down on a napkin for me, but the ink smeared when I set my iced tea down on it. I meant to ask her for it again, but I keep forgetting. \n\nAnyway, I tangled up the bridge schedule so badly that my husband finally had to step in. He's been doing all of it lately—making my calls, keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the rest of the repairs, which I always used to handle myself. I just couldn't keep track of who was coming when anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually drowsy or less alert than normal?\n\nPatient: Drowsy? No, I don't think so. I've always been an early riser, you know. Up with the birds! My husband used to joke that I was part rooster. When we lived in our first house on Elm Street, there actually was a rooster next door. Mr. Henderson owned it. He kept a whole coop of Rhode Island Reds right there in the city limits. I don't know how he got away with it, honestly. The zoning laws were much looser back then, I suppose. The rooster's name was Barnaby, and he would crow at the crack of dawn, every single day. It didn't bother me, though. I'd already be in the kitchen making coffee. I always used a percolator back then. The sound it made—that gentle bubbling—it's so much nicer than these modern drip machines that just hiss and spit at you. My percolator was stainless steel with a little glass knob on the top so you could see the coffee turning brown. \n\nWhat were you asking? Oh, about being drowsy. No, no, I'm quite alert most of the day.\n\nDoctor: What is today’s date?\n\nPatient: Today's date? Oh, let's see... well, I know it's a Tuesday, because the trash was collected this morning. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, making such a racket. It's one of those big automated ones, you know, with the mechanical arm that reaches out and grabs the bins? I preferred it when the men used to ride on the back of the truck and empty the cans by hand. They were always so polite. There was one young man, Tommy, I think his name was, who always used to wave at me if I was out watering the petunias. I used to grow the most beautiful petunias in the front window boxes. The secret is to pinch them back, you see, so they get bushy instead of leggy. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she never pinched hers, and they just hung down like sad little strings... \n\nBut the date... I'm sorry, I'm not entirely sure of the exact number today. I'd have to look at the calendar in the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words for common things when you speak?\n\nPatient: Words? No, I don't think I've had any trouble with that. I've always been quite a talker, you know. My mother used to say I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. We had a brass monkey doorstop when I was a little girl growing up in Ohio. It was so heavy, I used to stub my toe on it all the time running through the hallway. We had these beautiful hardwood floors, oak I think they were. My father used to wax them every Sunday afternoon until they shined like glass. He was very particular about those floors. He used a special paste wax that smelled like pine needles and turpentine. It was such a distinct smell. Every time I smell pine now, I think of him on his hands and knees with that old rag, buffing away... \n\nBut no, my words are just fine. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do there ever be times during the day when your thinking becomes much more confused, and then later clears up again?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, well, I don't think so. My mind stays pretty clear all day. I always say you have to use it or lose it, you know? That's why I do the crossword puzzle every morning. Just the one in the local paper, mind you, not the New York Times—those are a bit too tricky for me. Although my aunt Mildred, she used to do the Times crossword in pen! Can you imagine? She was a very smart woman, Mildred. She worked as a secretary for a judge downtown for thirty years. She took the bus every single day, wearing these lovely tailored suits with little matching hats. Women just don't wear hats anymore, do they? It's a real shame. You go to church now and people are wearing jeans. Jeans! I would never have dared step foot in the sanctuary wearing denim. My mother would have tanned my hide. She always made sure my sister and I had our Sunday best on, usually a nice floral print dress that she sewed herself on her old Singer machine. It had that heavy iron foot pedal... \n\nOh, but what were we talking about? Confused? No, no, my thinking doesn't get muddled up like that.\n\nDoctor: Around the time this started, did you begin any new medicines?\n\nPatient: New medicines? No, I haven't started anything new. I've been taking the same little white pills for my blood pressure for years now. Dr. Henderson prescribed them... oh, it must have been right after my husband passed. Dr. Henderson was such a wonderful man, he had the kindest eyes. Always wore a bowtie, too. You don't see doctors wearing bowties much anymore. They all wear those scrubs now, looking like they just rolled out of bed. Dr. Henderson always wore a crisp white coat and a different bowtie every time I saw him. He told me his wife collected them for him. They used to travel to Europe every summer, and she would find him a new silk tie in Paris or Rome. I've always wanted to go to Italy. I saw a special on the television once about the canals in Venice, and how the men sing while they row those little boats. Gondolas, I think they're called? It looked so romantic. My husband and I went to Niagara Falls for our honeymoon, which was lovely, of course, but it's not quite Venice, is it? We stayed in a little motel that had a heart-shaped tub... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no new medicines at all. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Can you still prepare a meal by yourself?\n\nPatient: Prepare a meal? Oh, well, I suppose I could, but my husband has been doing a lot of that lately. He makes a wonderful meatloaf. He uses crushed up Ritz crackers instead of breadcrumbs, which gives it such a buttery flavor. My mother used to use oatmeal in hers, which I never much cared for. It made it too chewy, you know? She grew up during the Depression, so she was always finding ways to stretch the meat. She would save bacon grease in a little tin coffee can on the stove. It was a Folgers can, I remember. The red one with the mountains on it. People don't save grease like that anymore, they just buy cooking oil in those big plastic jugs at the supermarket. The supermarket down the street from us just remodeled, actually. They put in a whole olive bar, with all different kinds of olives—green ones, black ones, ones stuffed with pimentos. I don't care for olives myself, they're too salty. \n\nBut really, my husband has had to take over a lot of things. I’ve just been... well, I’ve missed a few of my doctor visits lately, and I got the schedule for the senior center completely tangled up. I don't know how I did it, but I had myself going to bingo on the day they do water aerobics. I used to handle all the calendars and make the calls, even decide when we needed the plumber or the roofer, but he's had to step in and help with all of that now. I just can't seem to keep it straight.\n\nDoctor: Before these memory problems started, were you able to manage your own bills?\n\nPatient: Bills? Oh, yes, I used to do all the bills. Every month, right at the kitchen table. I had a special little calculator, one with the big buttons so I didn't have to squint. My grandson bought it for me. He works in computers now, you know. Makes a very good living, too. He lives out in California, near the ocean. I went to visit him once, and we drove down the coast. The water is so blue out there, not like the ocean here. We stopped at this little roadside stand and bought the biggest strawberries I've ever seen. They were the size of apples! And so sweet. I tried to grow strawberries in my garden once, but the rabbits kept getting to them before they were ripe. We have so many rabbits in the yard. I like to watch them from the window while I drink my morning tea. I always have Earl Grey, with just a splash of milk and half a lump of sugar. My mother used to drink it black, but I always found it too bitter that way. She had this beautiful china tea set with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I wonder whatever happened to that set... \n\nBut yes, before all this started, I did the bills just fine. Never missed a payment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? No, no, I know my way around just fine. We've lived in the same neighborhood for over forty years, so I know every single street. Although, they did just put in a new stop sign on the corner of Elm and Maple. I don't know why they needed a stop sign there, there's hardly any traffic. It's right in front of the old Miller house. They had a big golden retriever named Buster who used to chase the mail truck every single morning. The mailman, George, was such a good sport about it. He always kept little milk bones in his front pocket for the dogs on his route. You don't see mailmen doing that much anymore. Now they just drive those little white trucks and barely even get out to walk. George used to walk the whole route, carrying that heavy leather bag over his shoulder. I always wondered if that gave him back problems later in life. My husband has terrible back problems, you know. He has to sleep with a special pillow between his knees. We bought it from a catalog that came in the mail last spring. The catalog had all sorts of funny little gadgets in it, like a machine that peels apples for you. I prefer to peel my apples by hand with a little paring knife. My mother taught me how to do it in one long, continuous strip. She always said if the peel didn't break, it meant you were going to have good luck that day. \n\nBut anyway... no, to answer your question, I haven't been getting lost. I know exactly where I'm going.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to do the grocery shopping by yourself?\n\nPatient: Grocery shopping? Well, I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really tried to do a big trip all by myself lately. My husband usually drives us anyway because I don't like dealing with the parking lot at the grocery store. They repaved it last summer, and the lines are so bright yellow they practically hurt your eyes. Yellow is such a loud color, don't you think? My sister used to have a bright yellow sundress with little white daisies all over it. She wore it to a Fourth of July picnic back in... oh, it must have been 1965. We had the best potato salad at that picnic. Not the kind with the mayonnaise, but the German kind with the warm vinegar and bacon dressing. You have to use red potatoes for that, they hold their shape so much better than Russets. Russets are really only good for baking. I like to bake a potato and put a big dollop of sour cream and fresh chives on it. We used to grow chives in a little clay pot on the kitchen windowsill, but the cat kept chewing on them. He was an orange tabby named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he had a terrible habit of scratching the side of the sofa. We had to buy these special plastic covers for the armrests, which were just awful to sit on in the summer when you wore shorts because your skin would stick right to them... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the groceries. Yes, I'm just not sure if I could do a whole trip alone right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused while driving somewhere familiar?\n\nPatient: Confused while driving? Oh, well... I haven't driven in a little while, to be honest. My husband took the keys away after that business with the mailbox, which really wasn't my fault. The sun was right in my eyes. It was that time of day, you know, late afternoon when the sun hits the windshield just so, and you can't see a blessed thing. My father used to have these special clip-on sunglasses that he put over his regular glasses just for driving. They were a terrible shade of green, made him look like a giant bug! We used to tease him about it when we'd drive up to the lake for the summer. The lake house had this wonderful big wrap-around porch where we'd sit and string beans for dinner. We grew Kentucky Wonders, the pole beans, and they'd climb all the way up the trellis on the side of the house. You have to pick them when they're still young, otherwise they get tough and stringy, and nobody likes a stringy bean. I tried to show my daughter-in-law how to can them once, but she doesn't have the patience for it. She buys everything in cans from the supermarket. It's just not the same, is it? The tin gives everything a metallic taste... \n\nBut, what did you ask me? Oh, driving. I really haven't driven much lately, so I haven't had the chance to get confused.\n\nDoctor: Did you hit your head around the time these memory problems began?\n\nPatient: Hit my head? No, no, I don't think so. I haven't had a fall or anything like that. Although, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she took a terrible spill on her front steps last winter when it iced over. Broke her hip and had to go to that rehabilitation center over on 4th Street. The one with the brick front and the little garden in the courtyard. I went to visit her there and brought her some of my homemade banana bread. You know, the secret to good banana bread is you have to let the bananas get completely black on the outside. Most people throw them away when they look like that, but that's when they're the sweetest! I always add a handful of chopped walnuts to mine, too. My husband says it gives it a nice crunch. We used to have a big walnut tree in our backyard when we first got married. The squirrels would just go crazy for those nuts. They'd bury them all over the lawn, and in the spring, we'd have little walnut saplings popping up everywhere. My husband would get so frustrated trying to mow the lawn... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I haven't hit my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The sound it made—that gentle bubbling—it's so much nicer than these modern drip machines that just hiss and spit at you. My percolator was stainless steel with a little glass knob on the top so you could see the coffee turning brown. \n\nWhat were you asking? Oh, about being drowsy. No, no, I'm quite alert most of the day.\n\nDoctor: What is today’s date?\n\nPatient: Today's date? Oh, let's see... well, I know it's a Tuesday, because the trash was collected this morning. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, making such a racket. It's one of those big automated ones, you know, with the mechanical arm that reaches out and grabs the bins? I preferred it when the men used to ride on the back of the truck and empty the cans by hand. They were always so polite. There was one young man, Tommy, I think his name was, who always used to wave at me if I was out watering the petunias. I used to grow the most beautiful petunias in the front window boxes. The secret is to pinch them back, you see, so they get bushy instead of leggy. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she never pinched hers, and they just hung down like sad little strings... \n\nBut the date... I'm sorry, I'm not entirely sure of the exact number today. I'd have to look at the calendar in the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words for common things when you speak?\n\nPatient: Words? No, I don't think I've had any trouble with that. I've always been quite a talker, you know. My mother used to say I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. We had a brass monkey doorstop when I was a little girl growing up in Ohio. It was so heavy, I used to stub my toe on it all the time running through the hallway. We had these beautiful hardwood floors, oak I think they were. My father used to wax them every Sunday afternoon until they shined like glass. He was very particular about those floors. He used a special paste wax that smelled like pine needles and turpentine. It was such a distinct smell. Every time I smell pine now, I think of him on his hands and knees with that old rag, buffing away... \n\nBut no, my words are just fine. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do there ever be times during the day when your thinking becomes much more confused, and then later clears up again?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, well, I don't think so. My mind stays pretty clear all day. I always say you have to use it or lose it, you know? That's why I do the crossword puzzle every morning. Just the one in the local paper, mind you, not the New York Times—those are a bit too tricky for me. Although my aunt Mildred, she used to do the Times crossword in pen! Can you imagine? She was a very smart woman, Mildred. She worked as a secretary for a judge downtown for thirty years. She took the bus every single day, wearing these lovely tailored suits with little matching hats. Women just don't wear hats anymore, do they? It's a real shame. You go to church now and people are wearing jeans. Jeans! I would never have dared step foot in the sanctuary wearing denim. My mother would have tanned my hide. She always made sure my sister and I had our Sunday best on, usually a nice floral print dress that she sewed herself on her old Singer machine. It had that heavy iron foot pedal... \n\nOh, but what were we talking about? Confused? No, no, my thinking doesn't get muddled up like that.\n\nDoctor: Around the time this started, did you begin any new medicines?\n\nPatient: New medicines? No, I haven't started anything new. I've been taking the same little white pills for my blood pressure for years now. Dr. Henderson prescribed them... oh, it must have been right after my husband passed. Dr. Henderson was such a wonderful man, he had the kindest eyes. Always wore a bowtie, too. You don't see doctors wearing bowties much anymore. They all wear those scrubs now, looking like they just rolled out of bed. Dr. Henderson always wore a crisp white coat and a different bowtie every time I saw him. He told me his wife collected them for him. They used to travel to Europe every summer, and she would find him a new silk tie in Paris or Rome. I've always wanted to go to Italy. I saw a special on the television once about the canals in Venice, and how the men sing while they row those little boats. Gondolas, I think they're called? It looked so romantic. My husband and I went to Niagara Falls for our honeymoon, which was lovely, of course, but it's not quite Venice, is it? We stayed in a little motel that had a heart-shaped tub... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no new medicines at all. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Can you still prepare a meal by yourself?\n\nPatient: Prepare a meal? Oh, well, I suppose I could, but my husband has been doing a lot of that lately. He makes a wonderful meatloaf. He uses crushed up Ritz crackers instead of breadcrumbs, which gives it such a buttery flavor. My mother used to use oatmeal in hers, which I never much cared for. It made it too chewy, you know? She grew up during the Depression, so she was always finding ways to stretch the meat. She would save bacon grease in a little tin coffee can on the stove. It was a Folgers can, I remember. The red one with the mountains on it. People don't save grease like that anymore, they just buy cooking oil in those big plastic jugs at the supermarket. The supermarket down the street from us just remodeled, actually. They put in a whole olive bar, with all different kinds of olives—green ones, black ones, ones stuffed with pimentos. I don't care for olives myself, they're too salty. \n\nBut really, my husband has had to take over a lot of things. I’ve just been... well, I’ve missed a few of my doctor visits lately, and I got the schedule for the senior center completely tangled up. I don't know how I did it, but I had myself going to bingo on the day they do water aerobics. I used to handle all the calendars and make the calls, even decide when we needed the plumber or the roofer, but he's had to step in and help with all of that now. I just can't seem to keep it straight.\n\nDoctor: Before these memory problems started, were you able to manage your own bills?\n\nPatient: Bills? Oh, yes, I used to do all the bills. Every month, right at the kitchen table. I had a special little calculator, one with the big buttons so I didn't have to squint. My grandson bought it for me. He works in computers now, you know. Makes a very good living, too. He lives out in California, near the ocean. I went to visit him once, and we drove down the coast. The water is so blue out there, not like the ocean here. We stopped at this little roadside stand and bought the biggest strawberries I've ever seen. They were the size of apples! And so sweet. I tried to grow strawberries in my garden once, but the rabbits kept getting to them before they were ripe. We have so many rabbits in the yard. I like to watch them from the window while I drink my morning tea. I always have Earl Grey, with just a splash of milk and half a lump of sugar. My mother used to drink it black, but I always found it too bitter that way. She had this beautiful china tea set with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I wonder whatever happened to that set... \n\nBut yes, before all this started, I did the bills just fine. Never missed a payment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? No, no, I know my way around just fine. We've lived in the same neighborhood for over forty years, so I know every single street. Although, they did just put in a new stop sign on the corner of Elm and Maple. I don't know why they needed a stop sign there, there's hardly any traffic. It's right in front of the old Miller house. They had a big golden retriever named Buster who used to chase the mail truck every single morning. The mailman, George, was such a good sport about it. He always kept little milk bones in his front pocket for the dogs on his route. You don't see mailmen doing that much anymore. Now they just drive those little white trucks and barely even get out to walk. George used to walk the whole route, carrying that heavy leather bag over his shoulder. I always wondered if that gave him back problems later in life. My husband has terrible back problems, you know. He has to sleep with a special pillow between his knees. We bought it from a catalog that came in the mail last spring. The catalog had all sorts of funny little gadgets in it, like a machine that peels apples for you. I prefer to peel my apples by hand with a little paring knife. My mother taught me how to do it in one long, continuous strip. She always said if the peel didn't break, it meant you were going to have good luck that day. \n\nBut anyway... no, to answer your question, I haven't been getting lost. I know exactly where I'm going.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to do the grocery shopping by yourself?\n\nPatient: Grocery shopping? Well, I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really tried to do a big trip all by myself lately. My husband usually drives us anyway because I don't like dealing with the parking lot at the grocery store. They repaved it last summer, and the lines are so bright yellow they practically hurt your eyes. Yellow is such a loud color, don't you think? My sister used to have a bright yellow sundress with little white daisies all over it. She wore it to a Fourth of July picnic back in... oh, it must have been 1965. We had the best potato salad at that picnic. Not the kind with the mayonnaise, but the German kind with the warm vinegar and bacon dressing. You have to use red potatoes for that, they hold their shape so much better than Russets. Russets are really only good for baking. I like to bake a potato and put a big dollop of sour cream and fresh chives on it. We used to grow chives in a little clay pot on the kitchen windowsill, but the cat kept chewing on them. He was an orange tabby named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he had a terrible habit of scratching the side of the sofa. We had to buy these special plastic covers for the armrests, which were just awful to sit on in the summer when you wore shorts because your skin would stick right to them... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the groceries. Yes, I'm just not sure if I could do a whole trip alone right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused while driving somewhere familiar?\n\nPatient: Confused while driving? Oh, well... I haven't driven in a little while, to be honest. My husband took the keys away after that business with the mailbox, which really wasn't my fault. The sun was right in my eyes. It was that time of day, you know, late afternoon when the sun hits the windshield just so, and you can't see a blessed thing. My father used to have these special clip-on sunglasses that he put over his regular glasses just for driving. They were a terrible shade of green, made him look like a giant bug! We used to tease him about it when we'd drive up to the lake for the summer. The lake house had this wonderful big wrap-around porch where we'd sit and string beans for dinner. We grew Kentucky Wonders, the pole beans, and they'd climb all the way up the trellis on the side of the house. You have to pick them when they're still young, otherwise they get tough and stringy, and nobody likes a stringy bean. I tried to show my daughter-in-law how to can them once, but she doesn't have the patience for it. She buys everything in cans from the supermarket. It's just not the same, is it? The tin gives everything a metallic taste... \n\nBut, what did you ask me? Oh, driving. I really haven't driven much lately, so I haven't had the chance to get confused.\n\nDoctor: Did you hit your head around the time these memory problems began?\n\nPatient: Hit my head? No, no, I don't think so. I haven't had a fall or anything like that. Although, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she took a terrible spill on her front steps last winter when it iced over. Broke her hip and had to go to that rehabilitation center over on 4th Street. The one with the brick front and the little garden in the courtyard. I went to visit her there and brought her some of my homemade banana bread. You know, the secret to good banana bread is you have to let the bananas get completely black on the outside. Most people throw them away when they look like that, but that's when they're the sweetest! I always add a handful of chopped walnuts to mine, too. My husband says it gives it a nice crunch. We used to have a big walnut tree in our backyard when we first got married. The squirrels would just go crazy for those nuts. They'd bury them all over the lawn, and in the spring, we'd have little walnut saplings popping up everywhere. My husband would get so frustrated trying to mow the lawn... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I haven't hit my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

A decline? Well, yes, I suppose things have gotten a bit... messy lately. I've missed some doctor visits, which isn't like me at all, and I completely tangled up the schedule for the senior center. They have this wonderful water aerobics class, you see, and Gladys runs it. She always wears this bright pink swim cap that looks like a giant bubblegum bubble. She used to be a dancer, you know, back in her twenties, and she still has the most incredible posture. I always say good posture is half the battle when you get to our age. My mother made us walk around the parlor with books on our heads to keep our backs straight. Heavy books, too, like the dictionary. We had a beautiful leather-bound set of encyclopedias that a traveling salesman sold my father in 1952. It had gold lettering on the spine, and we used to look up pictures of exotic birds in the 'B' volume... 

But, oh, what was I saying? Right, the schedules. It got so bad that my husband has had to step in and help with all the phone calls and keeping the calendars straight. And the repair decisions, too! I used to handle all of that myself. We had a fellow come look at the gutters recently, and I just couldn't make heads or tails of what he was asking me to decide. He had such a loud truck, it rattled the front windows when he pulled into the driveway. But yes, my husband handles all those things now because I just can't seem to keep it all together like I used to.

I used to spend hours setting my hair in those little pink sponge rollers, you know the ones? You had to sleep in them, which was terribly uncomfortable, but my mother always told me you have to suffer for beauty... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about Susan?\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to manage your bills on your own?\n\nPatient: Well, the bills... oh, my husband has had to take over all of that lately. I used to handle everything, you know. The checkbook, the calendars, deciding when to call the plumber... I tangled up the senior center schedule so badly last month he just had to step in. It’s like I said about missing my doctor visits, I just can't keep it straight. \n\nSpeaking of the plumber, we had the most awful leak under the kitchen sink last Tuesday. The water just went everywhere, completely soaked the linoleum. We've had that same linoleum since 1985. It’s this lovely pale yellow color with little blue flowers on it. My mother hated it, she always said yellow belongs in a nursery, not a kitchen. But I always thought it looked so cheerful when the morning sun came through the window over the sink. I used to stand there washing dishes and watching the robins pull worms out of the front lawn. They're such clever little birds, robins. Did you know they can actually hear the worms moving underground? It’s true, I read it in a magazine once... a National Geographic, I think. Or maybe it was Reader's Digest. I used to have a subscription to Reader's Digest, they always had the best little jokes in the back...\n\nI'm sorry, dear, what did you ask me about? The checkbook? Yes, my husband does all of that now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or worried?\n\nPatient: Nervous? No, I wouldn't say nervous. I mean, everyone worries a little bit, don't they? Like when my grandson started driving. Oh, I was a wreck then! He bought this old beat-up Honda Civic, it was bright red. Well, it used to be bright red, the paint on the hood was peeling off in these big ugly flakes. He loved that car, though. He spent every weekend under the hood, getting grease all over his hands. His mother was always yelling at him about tracking the grease into the house. She had just put down this beautiful cream-colored carpet in the hallway. Cream! Can you imagine? With a teenager and two golden retrievers in the house? I told her it was a mistake, but she never listens to me. She’s stubborn, just like her father. My husband was the exact same way, once he made up his mind about something, that was it. Like the time he decided we were going to drive all the way to Mount Rushmore... \n\nOh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? You were asking if I feel nervous. No, dear, not really nervous. Just... foggy, like I said before.\n\nDoctor: Has this memory problem been getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Well, getting worse... I suppose it's been about four months now. It all started right around the time we had that terrible water damage. You know, from the leak under the sink I was telling you about? It ruined the cabinets, too, so we were pushed right into a remodel. \n\nAnd let me tell you, remodeling a kitchen is no joke. The dust! It gets everywhere, no matter how many plastic sheets they put up. We had these contractors in and out for weeks. The main fellow, Frank, he was a nice enough man, but he always wore these heavy work boots that left scuff marks all over the hardwood in the dining room. I tried to ask him to take them off, but my husband said it was a safety hazard for them to work in their socks. I suppose he was right, but it took me ages to get those scuffs out. I used to use this wonderful polish, Pledge, I think it was. The one in the yellow can that smells like lemons? They don't make it the same way anymore, though. Nothing smells quite like it used to. Even the lemons at the grocery store seem a bit... dull, don't you think? I remember when a lemon would just fill the whole kitchen with its scent...\n\nOh, dear, I've lost the thread again. What was it? Yes, four months. That's how long it's been like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with dressing or bathing?\n\nPatient: No, no, I can dress myself just fine. And bathe, yes. I still take my baths every evening. I've always preferred a bath to a shower, you know. Showers just feel so rushed. I like to fill the tub up nice and warm and use those little bath oil beads. Do you remember those? They used to come in all sorts of shapes, like little pearls or dolphins, and they'd melt in the hot water. My daughter bought me this big jar of them for Mother's Day back in... oh, it must have been 1998. It was a beautiful glass jar with a cork stopper. I kept it on the edge of the tub for years, even after the beads were all gone. I ended up putting cotton balls in it eventually. Cotton balls are so useful, aren't they? I use them for everything. Taking off makeup, cleaning the silver... my grandmother taught me that trick with the silver. She had this massive collection of silver spoons from all over the world. Every time someone went on a trip, they had to bring her back a spoon. She had one from Paris with a tiny little Eiffel Tower on the handle, and one from London with a guard in a red coat...\n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again, aren't I? You were asking about getting dressed. Yes, I manage all of that on my own.\n\nDoctor: Did this confusion start suddenly after the water leak and kitchen remodel?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Well, I don't know if I'd say suddenly. It's hard to put a finger on the exact day, you know? It just sort of... crept up on me while all that mess was happening. I remember standing in the kitchen, right in the middle of all that dust I told you about, trying to figure out where the contractor had moved my good mixing bowls. They were these beautiful Pyrex bowls, the vintage ones? Primary colors. The large one was yellow, then green, red, and the littlest one was blue. I got them as a wedding present from my Aunt Clara. She was such a practical woman. Always gave gifts you could actually use, not like those decorative porcelain figurines my mother-in-law used to collect. She had dozens of those little shepherdesses taking up every inch of the mantelpiece. Dust collectors, I called them. And they were a nightmare to clean. You had to take a little soft-bristled toothbrush to get into all the tiny folds of their porcelain dresses... \n\nOh, what was I saying? The confusion. Yes, I'm just not sure if it was sudden. It just seemed to happen around that whole kitchen ordeal.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop driving because of these memory problems?\n\nPatient: Driving? Oh, well, I still drive a little bit, mostly just to the grocery store and back. But it's all the other things that have gotten so tangled up. I missed two doctor's appointments last month, just completely forgot them. And the schedule at the senior center... I used to organize the Tuesday bridge club, you know. We’ve been playing for fifteen years. We play Chicago style, which I always say is much more forgiving if you draw a bad partner. My friend Helen, bless her heart, she bids like she’s got eyes in the back of her head, but she can’t remember what trump is half the time. She always brings these little lemon bars to the games, though. The ones with the powdered sugar on top? They are just divine. I asked her for the recipe once, and she wrote it down on a napkin for me, but the ink smeared when I set my iced tea down on it. I meant to ask her for it again, but I keep forgetting. \n\nAnyway, I tangled up the bridge schedule so badly that my husband finally had to step in. He's been doing all of it lately—making my calls, keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the rest of the repairs, which I always used to handle myself. I just couldn't keep track of who was coming when anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually drowsy or less alert than normal?\n\nPatient: Drowsy? No, I don't think so. I've always been an early riser, you know. Up with the birds! My husband used to joke that I was part rooster. When we lived in our first house on Elm Street, there actually was a rooster next door. Mr. Henderson owned it. He kept a whole coop of Rhode Island Reds right there in the city limits. I don't know how he got away with it, honestly. The zoning laws were much looser back then, I suppose. The rooster's name was Barnaby, and he would crow at the crack of dawn, every single day. It didn't bother me, though. I'd already be in the kitchen making coffee. I always used a percolator back then. The sound it made—that gentle bubbling—it's so much nicer than these modern drip machines that just hiss and spit at you. My percolator was stainless steel with a little glass knob on the top so you could see the coffee turning brown. \n\nWhat were you asking? Oh, about being drowsy. No, no, I'm quite alert most of the day.\n\nDoctor: What is today’s date?\n\nPatient: Today's date? Oh, let's see... well, I know it's a Tuesday, because the trash was collected this morning. The garbage truck always comes so early on Tuesdays, making such a racket. It's one of those big automated ones, you know, with the mechanical arm that reaches out and grabs the bins? I preferred it when the men used to ride on the back of the truck and empty the cans by hand. They were always so polite. There was one young man, Tommy, I think his name was, who always used to wave at me if I was out watering the petunias. I used to grow the most beautiful petunias in the front window boxes. The secret is to pinch them back, you see, so they get bushy instead of leggy. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she never pinched hers, and they just hung down like sad little strings... \n\nBut the date... I'm sorry, I'm not entirely sure of the exact number today. I'd have to look at the calendar in the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words for common things when you speak?\n\nPatient: Words? No, I don't think I've had any trouble with that. I've always been quite a talker, you know. My mother used to say I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. We had a brass monkey doorstop when I was a little girl growing up in Ohio. It was so heavy, I used to stub my toe on it all the time running through the hallway. We had these beautiful hardwood floors, oak I think they were. My father used to wax them every Sunday afternoon until they shined like glass. He was very particular about those floors. He used a special paste wax that smelled like pine needles and turpentine. It was such a distinct smell. Every time I smell pine now, I think of him on his hands and knees with that old rag, buffing away... \n\nBut no, my words are just fine. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do there ever be times during the day when your thinking becomes much more confused, and then later clears up again?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, well, I don't think so. My mind stays pretty clear all day. I always say you have to use it or lose it, you know? That's why I do the crossword puzzle every morning. Just the one in the local paper, mind you, not the New York Times—those are a bit too tricky for me. Although my aunt Mildred, she used to do the Times crossword in pen! Can you imagine? She was a very smart woman, Mildred. She worked as a secretary for a judge downtown for thirty years. She took the bus every single day, wearing these lovely tailored suits with little matching hats. Women just don't wear hats anymore, do they? It's a real shame. You go to church now and people are wearing jeans. Jeans! I would never have dared step foot in the sanctuary wearing denim. My mother would have tanned my hide. She always made sure my sister and I had our Sunday best on, usually a nice floral print dress that she sewed herself on her old Singer machine. It had that heavy iron foot pedal... \n\nOh, but what were we talking about? Confused? No, no, my thinking doesn't get muddled up like that.\n\nDoctor: Around the time this started, did you begin any new medicines?\n\nPatient: New medicines? No, I haven't started anything new. I've been taking the same little white pills for my blood pressure for years now. Dr. Henderson prescribed them... oh, it must have been right after my husband passed. Dr. Henderson was such a wonderful man, he had the kindest eyes. Always wore a bowtie, too. You don't see doctors wearing bowties much anymore. They all wear those scrubs now, looking like they just rolled out of bed. Dr. Henderson always wore a crisp white coat and a different bowtie every time I saw him. He told me his wife collected them for him. They used to travel to Europe every summer, and she would find him a new silk tie in Paris or Rome. I've always wanted to go to Italy. I saw a special on the television once about the canals in Venice, and how the men sing while they row those little boats. Gondolas, I think they're called? It looked so romantic. My husband and I went to Niagara Falls for our honeymoon, which was lovely, of course, but it's not quite Venice, is it? We stayed in a little motel that had a heart-shaped tub... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no new medicines at all. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Can you still prepare a meal by yourself?\n\nPatient: Prepare a meal? Oh, well, I suppose I could, but my husband has been doing a lot of that lately. He makes a wonderful meatloaf. He uses crushed up Ritz crackers instead of breadcrumbs, which gives it such a buttery flavor. My mother used to use oatmeal in hers, which I never much cared for. It made it too chewy, you know? She grew up during the Depression, so she was always finding ways to stretch the meat. She would save bacon grease in a little tin coffee can on the stove. It was a Folgers can, I remember. The red one with the mountains on it. People don't save grease like that anymore, they just buy cooking oil in those big plastic jugs at the supermarket. The supermarket down the street from us just remodeled, actually. They put in a whole olive bar, with all different kinds of olives—green ones, black ones, ones stuffed with pimentos. I don't care for olives myself, they're too salty. \n\nBut really, my husband has had to take over a lot of things. I’ve just been... well, I’ve missed a few of my doctor visits lately, and I got the schedule for the senior center completely tangled up. I don't know how I did it, but I had myself going to bingo on the day they do water aerobics. I used to handle all the calendars and make the calls, even decide when we needed the plumber or the roofer, but he's had to step in and help with all of that now. I just can't seem to keep it straight.\n\nDoctor: Before these memory problems started, were you able to manage your own bills?\n\nPatient: Bills? Oh, yes, I used to do all the bills. Every month, right at the kitchen table. I had a special little calculator, one with the big buttons so I didn't have to squint. My grandson bought it for me. He works in computers now, you know. Makes a very good living, too. He lives out in California, near the ocean. I went to visit him once, and we drove down the coast. The water is so blue out there, not like the ocean here. We stopped at this little roadside stand and bought the biggest strawberries I've ever seen. They were the size of apples! And so sweet. I tried to grow strawberries in my garden once, but the rabbits kept getting to them before they were ripe. We have so many rabbits in the yard. I like to watch them from the window while I drink my morning tea. I always have Earl Grey, with just a splash of milk and half a lump of sugar. My mother used to drink it black, but I always found it too bitter that way. She had this beautiful china tea set with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I wonder whatever happened to that set... \n\nBut yes, before all this started, I did the bills just fine. Never missed a payment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that used to be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? No, no, I know my way around just fine. We've lived in the same neighborhood for over forty years, so I know every single street. Although, they did just put in a new stop sign on the corner of Elm and Maple. I don't know why they needed a stop sign there, there's hardly any traffic. It's right in front of the old Miller house. They had a big golden retriever named Buster who used to chase the mail truck every single morning. The mailman, George, was such a good sport about it. He always kept little milk bones in his front pocket for the dogs on his route. You don't see mailmen doing that much anymore. Now they just drive those little white trucks and barely even get out to walk. George used to walk the whole route, carrying that heavy leather bag over his shoulder. I always wondered if that gave him back problems later in life. My husband has terrible back problems, you know. He has to sleep with a special pillow between his knees. We bought it from a catalog that came in the mail last spring. The catalog had all sorts of funny little gadgets in it, like a machine that peels apples for you. I prefer to peel my apples by hand with a little paring knife. My mother taught me how to do it in one long, continuous strip. She always said if the peel didn't break, it meant you were going to have good luck that day. \n\nBut anyway... no, to answer your question, I haven't been getting lost. I know exactly where I'm going.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to do the grocery shopping by yourself?\n\nPatient: Grocery shopping? Well, I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really tried to do a big trip all by myself lately. My husband usually drives us anyway because I don't like dealing with the parking lot at the grocery store. They repaved it last summer, and the lines are so bright yellow they practically hurt your eyes. Yellow is such a loud color, don't you think? My sister used to have a bright yellow sundress with little white daisies all over it. She wore it to a Fourth of July picnic back in... oh, it must have been 1965. We had the best potato salad at that picnic. Not the kind with the mayonnaise, but the German kind with the warm vinegar and bacon dressing. You have to use red potatoes for that, they hold their shape so much better than Russets. Russets are really only good for baking. I like to bake a potato and put a big dollop of sour cream and fresh chives on it. We used to grow chives in a little clay pot on the kitchen windowsill, but the cat kept chewing on them. He was an orange tabby named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he had a terrible habit of scratching the side of the sofa. We had to buy these special plastic covers for the armrests, which were just awful to sit on in the summer when you wore shorts because your skin would stick right to them... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the groceries. Yes, I'm just not sure if I could do a whole trip alone right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused while driving somewhere familiar?\n\nPatient: Confused while driving? Oh, well... I haven't driven in a little while, to be honest. My husband took the keys away after that business with the mailbox, which really wasn't my fault. The sun was right in my eyes. It was that time of day, you know, late afternoon when the sun hits the windshield just so, and you can't see a blessed thing. My father used to have these special clip-on sunglasses that he put over his regular glasses just for driving. They were a terrible shade of green, made him look like a giant bug! We used to tease him about it when we'd drive up to the lake for the summer. The lake house had this wonderful big wrap-around porch where we'd sit and string beans for dinner. We grew Kentucky Wonders, the pole beans, and they'd climb all the way up the trellis on the side of the house. You have to pick them when they're still young, otherwise they get tough and stringy, and nobody likes a stringy bean. I tried to show my daughter-in-law how to can them once, but she doesn't have the patience for it. She buys everything in cans from the supermarket. It's just not the same, is it? The tin gives everything a metallic taste... \n\nBut, what did you ask me? Oh, driving. I really haven't driven much lately, so I haven't had the chance to get confused.\n\nDoctor: Did you hit your head around the time these memory problems began?\n\nPatient: Hit my head? No, no, I don't think so. I haven't had a fall or anything like that. Although, my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she took a terrible spill on her front steps last winter when it iced over. Broke her hip and had to go to that rehabilitation center over on 4th Street. The one with the brick front and the little garden in the courtyard. I went to visit her there and brought her some of my homemade banana bread. You know, the secret to good banana bread is you have to let the bananas get completely black on the outside. Most people throw them away when they look like that, but that's when they're the sweetest! I always add a handful of chopped walnuts to mine, too. My husband says it gives it a nice crunch. We used to have a big walnut tree in our backyard when we first got married. The squirrels would just go crazy for those nuts. They'd bury them all over the lawn, and in the spring, we'd have little walnut saplings popping up everywhere. My husband would get so frustrated trying to mow the lawn... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I haven't hit my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your memory trouble caused a clear decline from how well you used to manage your daily life?\n\nPatient: A decline? Well, yes, I suppose things have gotten a bit... messy lately. I've missed some doctor visits, which isn't like me at all, and I completely tangled up the schedule for the senior center. They have this wonderful water aerobics class, you see, and Gladys runs it. She always wears this bright pink swim cap that looks like a giant bubblegum bubble. She used to be a dancer, you know, back in her twenties, and she still has the most incredible posture. I always say good posture is half the battle when you get to our age. My mother made us walk around the parlor with books on our heads to keep our backs straight. Heavy books, too, like the dictionary. We had a beautiful leather-bound set of encyclopedias that a traveling salesman sold my father in 1952. It had gold lettering on the spine, and we used to look up pictures of exotic birds in the 'B' volume... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the schedules. It got so bad that my husband has had to step in and help with all the phone calls and keeping the calendars straight. And the repair decisions, too! I used to handle all of that myself. We had a fellow come look at the gutters recently, and I just couldn't make heads or tails of what he was asking me to decide. He had such a loud truck, it rattled the front windows when he pulled into the driveway. But yes, my husband handles all those things now because I just can't seem to keep it all together like I used to."
  }
